People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, nice to meet you, Doc. I guess... well, to be honest, it’s a bit of a story. You see, I retired recently—finally decided it was time to step away after all those years—and everybody always tells you that retirement is about taking it easy, right? Sitting on the porch, maybe doing a little fishing, just enjoying the quiet. 

But what brings me in is that since I stopped working, I just... I can’t seem to slow down. Not even a little bit. I wake up and I'm just up and moving from one little job to the next all day long. First I'm out cleaning the gutters, then I'm in the garage reorganizing the workbenches, then I'm fixing a squeaky hinge on the back gate that wasn't even really bothering anybody. 

It’s just... it's like I still have a crew waiting on me, you know? Like if I don't keep moving and getting things done, the whole site is gonna fall behind schedule. But there is no schedule anymore! I just go and go and go, and I can't seem to just sit in my chair for ten minutes without feeling like I need to be up doing something. It's driving me a little crazy, to be honest with you, so I figured I better come in and talk to somebody about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I've been feeling unusually upbeat or anything like that. It's not like I'm walking around on cloud nine or feeling like I won the lottery. And as for being overly energetic... well, I guess that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because like I said, I'm always on the move, always finding some project to tackle. But it's not this... this burst of extra energy, you know? It's just my normal speed. It's how I've always been, just... go, go, go. 

And irritable? Well, my wife might tell you a different story if she was sitting here, but honestly, I don't think I'm any more irritable than usual. Sure, I get frustrated when things don't go right, like when I was trying to fix that hinge and the screws were all stripped out. I might have muttered a few choice words then, but who wouldn't? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. Actually, if anything, I'm probably the opposite. I've always been the guy making the calls, you know? When you spend thirty-some years running a crew, you can't be standing around waiting for someone else to tell you what size nail to use or whether to pour the concrete today or tomorrow. You just look at the sky, look at the plans, and you make the call. 

Even now around the house, I don't need my wife to tell me how to organize the garage or what color to paint the trim. I just go ahead and do it. Sometimes that gets me in a little hot water, I suppose—like last week when I decided to trim back the oak tree in the front yard without mentioning it to her first. She came home from the grocery store and just about had a fit because she thought I took off too many branches on the left side. But I told her, "Look, that branch was getting too close to the power lines, and it's better to be safe than sorry." So, no, I definitely don't need reassurance. I just make a decision and go with it, for better or worse. That's never been a problem for me.

I wake up and I'm just up and moving from one little job to the next all day long. First I'm out cleaning the gutters, then I'm in the garage reorganizing the workbenches, then I'm fixing a squeaky hinge on the back gate that wasn't even really bothering anybody. \n\nIt’s just... it's like I still have a crew waiting on me, you know? Like if I don't keep moving and getting things done, the whole site is gonna fall behind schedule. But there is no schedule anymore! I just go and go and go, and I can't seem to just sit in my chair for ten minutes without feeling like I need to be up doing something. It's driving me a little crazy, to be honest with you, so I figured I better come in and talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually upbeat, overly energetic, or much more irritable than is normal for you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I've been feeling unusually upbeat or anything like that. It's not like I'm walking around on cloud nine or feeling like I won the lottery. And as for being overly energetic... well, I guess that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because like I said, I'm always on the move, always finding some project to tackle. But it's not this... this burst of extra energy, you know? It's just my normal speed. It's how I've always been, just... go, go, go. \n\nAnd irritable? Well, my wife might tell you a different story if she was sitting here, but honestly, I don't think I'm any more irritable than usual. Sure, I get frustrated when things don't go right, like when I was trying to fix that hinge and the screws were all stripped out. I might have muttered a few choice words then, but who wouldn't? It's not like I'm snapping at people for no reason or flying off the handle over every little thing. I'm just... well, I'm just me, you know? Just trying to stay busy and get things done, even if there really isn't anything that *needs* getting done. It's just a restless feeling, not like I'm suddenly a different person or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to make everyday decisions without getting advice or reassurance from other people?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. Actually, if anything, I'm probably the opposite. I've always been the guy making the calls, you know? When you spend thirty-some years running a crew, you can't be standing around waiting for someone else to tell you what size nail to use or whether to pour the concrete today or tomorrow. You just look at the sky, look at the plans, and you make the call. \n\nEven now around the house, I don't need my wife to tell me how to organize the garage or what color to paint the trim. I just go ahead and do it. Sometimes that gets me in a little hot water, I suppose—like last week when I decided to trim back the oak tree in the front yard without mentioning it to her first. She came home from the grocery store and just about had a fit because she thought I took off too many branches on the left side. But I told her, \"Look, that branch was getting too close to the power lines, and it's better to be safe than sorry.\" So, no, I definitely don't need reassurance. I just make a decision and go with it, for better or worse. That's never been a problem for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, I’ve always been a guy who likes to keep busy, always had a lot of energy, you know? Like I said, running a crew, you gotta be moving. But... this specific thing, this feeling like I'm just spinning my wheels and can't turn the engine off? That's newer. I'd say it really became a real problem, like, a noticeable issue where I'm driving myself and my wife up the wall, after I retired. 

I hung up my toolbelt—well, my professional toolbelt, anyway, I still got my own out in the garage—about a year and two months ago. It was late October, I remember because we had just finished up a big siding job over on Elm Street right before the first frost hit. And I thought, "That's it, I'm done. Time to relax." But almost right away, maybe a couple weeks in, once the novelty of not having an alarm clock wore off, that's when this really kicked into high gear. It's like, without the job to channel all that into, it just started spilling out everywhere else. So yeah, I've always been a go-getter, but this... this inability to just sit still, this is really since I retired a year and two months ago.

I mean, I wouldn't say I've been feeling unusually upbeat or anything like that. It's not like I'm walking around on cloud nine or feeling like I won the lottery. And as for being overly energetic... well, I guess that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because like I said, I'm always on the move, always finding some project to tackle. But it's not this... this burst of extra energy, you know? It's just my normal speed. It's how I've always been, just... go, go, go. \n\nAnd irritable? Well, my wife might tell you a different story if she was sitting here, but honestly, I don't think I'm any more irritable than usual. Sure, I get frustrated when things don't go right, like when I was trying to fix that hinge and the screws were all stripped out. I might have muttered a few choice words then, but who wouldn't? It's not like I'm snapping at people for no reason or flying off the handle over every little thing. I'm just... well, I'm just me, you know? Just trying to stay busy and get things done, even if there really isn't anything that *needs* getting done. It's just a restless feeling, not like I'm suddenly a different person or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to make everyday decisions without getting advice or reassurance from other people?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. Actually, if anything, I'm probably the opposite. I've always been the guy making the calls, you know? When you spend thirty-some years running a crew, you can't be standing around waiting for someone else to tell you what size nail to use or whether to pour the concrete today or tomorrow. You just look at the sky, look at the plans, and you make the call. \n\nEven now around the house, I don't need my wife to tell me how to organize the garage or what color to paint the trim. I just go ahead and do it. Sometimes that gets me in a little hot water, I suppose—like last week when I decided to trim back the oak tree in the front yard without mentioning it to her first. She came home from the grocery store and just about had a fit because she thought I took off too many branches on the left side. But I told her, \"Look, that branch was getting too close to the power lines, and it's better to be safe than sorry.\" So, no, I definitely don't need reassurance. I just make a decision and go with it, for better or worse. That's never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember, or did this start more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I’ve always been a guy who likes to keep busy, always had a lot of energy, you know? Like I said, running a crew, you gotta be moving. But... this specific thing, this feeling like I'm just spinning my wheels and can't turn the engine off? That's newer. I'd say it really became a real problem, like, a noticeable issue where I'm driving myself and my wife up the wall, after I retired. \n\nI hung up my toolbelt—well, my professional toolbelt, anyway, I still got my own out in the garage—about a year and two months ago. It was late October, I remember because we had just finished up a big siding job over on Elm Street right before the first frost hit. And I thought, \"That's it, I'm done. Time to relax.\" But almost right away, maybe a couple weeks in, once the novelty of not having an alarm clock wore off, that's when this really kicked into high gear. It's like, without the job to channel all that into, it just started spilling out everywhere else. So yeah, I've always been a go-getter, but this... this inability to just sit still, this is really since I retired a year and two months ago.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, managing things on my own is basically what I've done my whole life. When you're out there on a job site, you can't be wringing your hands worrying if you can handle it. You just handle it. You figure out how many yards of concrete you need, you make sure the framing is plumb, you deal with the inspector when he shows up in a bad mood... you just do it. 

Even now, around the house, I'm not sitting there worrying if I can manage. If the lawnmower won't start, I don't panic and think, "Oh no, I can't do this." I just pull the spark plug, check the carburetor, maybe run out to the hardware store for some fresh gas or a new filter. I mean, I might get frustrated if I have to take the whole housing apart just to get to one little bolt—they don't make these things like they used to, let me tell you, it's all plastic clips now that snap if you look at them wrong—but I don't worry that I *can't* do it. 

I guess the problem isn't worrying about managing things, it's that I'm trying to manage *too many* things all at once because I can't just leave well enough alone. My wife says I'm managing things that don't need managing. Like last week, I decided to reorganize the entire basement storage area. Did it need doing? Maybe not right then. But I just started pulling boxes out, sorting through old holiday decorations, tossing out stuff we haven't used since the kids were in grade school. I had a whole system going. So, no, I definitely don't worry about being able to handle things on my own. I've always been independent that way.

No, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Not depressed, anyway. Frustrated, maybe, like I said, because I just can't seem to get comfortable doing nothing, but not sad. 

I mean, I look at some of the guys I used to work with who retired before me. Take Charlie, for instance. He retired about five years ago, and within six months, he was just sitting in his recliner all day watching cable news, getting all worked up about politics, and just looking miserable. I went over to his place to help him fix a leaky pipe under his sink—he never was any good at plumbing, always a framing guy—and he just looked so defeated, you know? Like all the wind was out of his sails. 

I'm not like that. I wake up, and I want to go, go, go. I want to build something, fix something, tear something down and put it back together better. My mood is fine, honestly. Sometimes I even wake up in a great mood because I've got a whole list of projects in my head. Like yesterday, I woke up thinking about how I could build a better shelving unit for my wife's gardening supplies out back. I had the whole thing sketched out on a napkin by the time she came down for coffee. So, no, I'm not sitting around crying into my oatmeal or feeling hopeless. It's more like... I've got all this energy and nowhere productive to put it, which drives me a little crazy, sure, but I'm definitely not depressed. I just need a project. Always need a project.

You just look at the sky, look at the plans, and you make the call. \n\nEven now around the house, I don't need my wife to tell me how to organize the garage or what color to paint the trim. I just go ahead and do it. Sometimes that gets me in a little hot water, I suppose—like last week when I decided to trim back the oak tree in the front yard without mentioning it to her first. She came home from the grocery store and just about had a fit because she thought I took off too many branches on the left side. But I told her, \"Look, that branch was getting too close to the power lines, and it's better to be safe than sorry.\" So, no, I definitely don't need reassurance. I just make a decision and go with it, for better or worse. That's never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember, or did this start more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I’ve always been a guy who likes to keep busy, always had a lot of energy, you know? Like I said, running a crew, you gotta be moving. But... this specific thing, this feeling like I'm just spinning my wheels and can't turn the engine off? That's newer. I'd say it really became a real problem, like, a noticeable issue where I'm driving myself and my wife up the wall, after I retired. \n\nI hung up my toolbelt—well, my professional toolbelt, anyway, I still got my own out in the garage—about a year and two months ago. It was late October, I remember because we had just finished up a big siding job over on Elm Street right before the first frost hit. And I thought, \"That's it, I'm done. Time to relax.\" But almost right away, maybe a couple weeks in, once the novelty of not having an alarm clock wore off, that's when this really kicked into high gear. It's like, without the job to channel all that into, it just started spilling out everywhere else. So yeah, I've always been a go-getter, but this... this inability to just sit still, this is really since I retired a year and two months ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about being unable to manage things on your own?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, managing things on my own is basically what I've done my whole life. When you're out there on a job site, you can't be wringing your hands worrying if you can handle it. You just handle it. You figure out how many yards of concrete you need, you make sure the framing is plumb, you deal with the inspector when he shows up in a bad mood... you just do it. \n\nEven now, around the house, I'm not sitting there worrying if I can manage. If the lawnmower won't start, I don't panic and think, \"Oh no, I can't do this.\" I just pull the spark plug, check the carburetor, maybe run out to the hardware store for some fresh gas or a new filter. I mean, I might get frustrated if I have to take the whole housing apart just to get to one little bolt—they don't make these things like they used to, let me tell you, it's all plastic clips now that snap if you look at them wrong—but I don't worry that I *can't* do it. \n\nI guess the problem isn't worrying about managing things, it's that I'm trying to manage *too many* things all at once because I can't just leave well enough alone. My wife says I'm managing things that don't need managing. Like last week, I decided to reorganize the entire basement storage area. Did it need doing? Maybe not right then. But I just started pulling boxes out, sorting through old holiday decorations, tossing out stuff we haven't used since the kids were in grade school. I had a whole system going. So, no, I definitely don't worry about being able to handle things on my own. I've always been independent that way.\n\nDoctor: Since retiring, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Not depressed, anyway. Frustrated, maybe, like I said, because I just can't seem to get comfortable doing nothing, but not sad. \n\nI mean, I look at some of the guys I used to work with who retired before me. Take Charlie, for instance. He retired about five years ago, and within six months, he was just sitting in his recliner all day watching cable news, getting all worked up about politics, and just looking miserable. I went over to his place to help him fix a leaky pipe under his sink—he never was any good at plumbing, always a framing guy—and he just looked so defeated, you know? Like all the wind was out of his sails. \n\nI'm not like that. I wake up, and I want to go, go, go. I want to build something, fix something, tear something down and put it back together better. My mood is fine, honestly. Sometimes I even wake up in a great mood because I've got a whole list of projects in my head. Like yesterday, I woke up thinking about how I could build a better shelving unit for my wife's gardening supplies out back. I had the whole thing sketched out on a napkin by the time she came down for coffee. So, no, I'm not sitting around crying into my oatmeal or feeling hopeless. It's more like... I've got all this energy and nowhere productive to put it, which drives me a little crazy, sure, but I'm definitely not depressed. I just need a project. Always need a project.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

My mood is fine, honestly. Sometimes I even wake up in a great mood because I've got a whole list of projects in my head. Like yesterday, I woke up thinking about how I could build a better shelving unit for my wife's gardening supplies out back. I had the whole thing sketched out on a napkin by the time she came down for coffee. So, no, I'm not sitting around crying into my oatmeal or feeling hopeless. It's more like... I've got all this energy and nowhere productive to put it, which drives me a little crazy, sure, but I'm definitely not depressed. I just need a project. Always need a project.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel inferior to other people?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. Inferior? Nah. I mean, look, I've worked hard my whole life. I know what I bring to the table, you know? When you've spent forty years in construction, you learn pretty quick that everybody's got their own set of skills. 

Like, take my brother-in-law, Dave. Dave's an accountant. He's got all these fancy degrees, wears a suit to work, the whole nine yards. And sure, if you put me in front of a spreadsheet, I'd probably be lost in five minutes. But if Dave's roof starts leaking, or his deck starts sagging? He's calling me. He doesn't know a joist hanger from a hole in the ground. So, do I feel inferior to Dave because he makes more money or went to college? Absolutely not. We just do different things. 

I've always been proud of the work I do. I built my own garage from the ground up, poured the slab myself, framed it, wired it—well, I had a buddy check the electrical, just to be safe, but I did the work. You don't do something like that and feel inferior to the guy next door just because he drives a newer truck. 

I guess what I'm saying is, I know my worth. I know I can out-work most guys half my age, even now. Honestly, if anything, I get a little impatient with people who *don't* know how to do things, or who just hire everything out. It’s like, come on, pick up a hammer, figure it out! So no, I don't walk around feeling less than anybody else. I’m just a guy who likes to get things done, and I know I’m damn good at getting things done. It’s just... getting myself to stop doing things that’s the problem right now. But inferior? No way.

Actually, if anything, I'm probably the opposite. I've always been the guy making the calls, you know? I just make a decision and go with it, for better or worse. That's never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember, or did this start more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I’ve always been a guy who likes to keep busy, always had a lot of energy, you know? You figure out how many yards of concrete you need, you make sure the framing is plumb, you deal with the inspector when he shows up in a bad mood... you just do it. \n\nEven now, around the house, I'm not sitting there worrying if I can manage. job to the next all day like I still have a crew waiting on me.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: In the storytelling meetings I’ll answer or jump in before the other person finishes, then realize I cut them off and feel bad about it.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the hardware store or sitting for my turn at the clinic makes me antsy, and I catch myself checking the time, shifting around, and wanting to hurry things along.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even back in grade school, my teachers said I was bright but scattered, always losing pencils, calling out answers, and needing reminders to finish my work before recess.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My wife has had to take over keeping the calendar and checking behind me, and I’ve had to apologize to the community center and garden group more than once for letting people down.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This became a real problem after I retired from handyman work about a year and two months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about being unable to manage things on your own?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, managing things on my own is basically what I've done my whole life. When you're out there on a job site, you can't be wringing your hands worrying if you can handle it. You just handle it. You figure out how many yards of concrete you need, you make sure the framing is plumb, you deal with the inspector when he shows up in a bad mood... you just do it. \n\nEven now, around the house, I'm not sitting there worrying if I can manage. If the lawnmower won't start, I don't panic and think, \"Oh no, I can't do this.\" I just pull the spark plug, check the carburetor, maybe run out to the hardware store for some fresh gas or a new filter. I mean, I might get frustrated if I have to take the whole housing apart just to get to one little bolt—they don't make these things like they used to, let me tell you, it's all plastic clips now that snap if you look at them wrong—but I don't worry that I *can't* do it. \n\nI guess the problem isn't worrying about managing things, it's that I'm trying to manage *too many* things all at once because I can't just leave well enough alone. My wife says I'm managing things that don't need managing. Like last week, I decided to reorganize the entire basement storage area. Did it need doing? Maybe not right then. But I just started pulling boxes out, sorting through old holiday decorations, tossing out stuff we haven't used since the kids were in grade school. I had a whole system going. So, no, I definitely don't worry about being able to handle things on my own. I've always been independent that way.\n\nDoctor: Since retiring, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Not depressed, anyway. Frustrated, maybe, like I said, because I just can't seem to get comfortable doing nothing, but not sad. \n\nI mean, I look at some of the guys I used to work with who retired before me. Take Charlie, for instance. He retired about five years ago, and within six months, he was just sitting in his recliner all day watching cable news, getting all worked up about politics, and just looking miserable. I went over to his place to help him fix a leaky pipe under his sink—he never was any good at plumbing, always a framing guy—and he just looked so defeated, you know? Like all the wind was out of his sails. \n\nI'm not like that. I wake up, and I want to go, go, go. I want to build something, fix something, tear something down and put it back together better. My mood is fine, honestly. Sometimes I even wake up in a great mood because I've got a whole list of projects in my head. Like yesterday, I woke up thinking about how I could build a better shelving unit for my wife's gardening supplies out back. I had the whole thing sketched out on a napkin by the time she came down for coffee. So, no, I'm not sitting around crying into my oatmeal or feeling hopeless. It's more like... I've got all this energy and nowhere productive to put it, which drives me a little crazy, sure, but I'm definitely not depressed. I just need a project. Always need a project.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel inferior to other people?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. Inferior? Nah. I mean, look, I've worked hard my whole life. I know what I bring to the table, you know? When you've spent forty years in construction, you learn pretty quick that everybody's got their own set of skills. \n\nLike, take my brother-in-law, Dave. Dave's an accountant. He's got all these fancy degrees, wears a suit to work, the whole nine yards. And sure, if you put me in front of a spreadsheet, I'd probably be lost in five minutes. But if Dave's roof starts leaking, or his deck starts sagging? He's calling me. He doesn't know a joist hanger from a hole in the ground. So, do I feel inferior to Dave because he makes more money or went to college? Absolutely not. We just do different things. \n\nI've always been proud of the work I do. I built my own garage from the ground up, poured the slab myself, framed it, wired it—well, I had a buddy check the electrical, just to be safe, but I did the work. You don't do something like that and feel inferior to the guy next door just because he drives a newer truck. \n\nI guess what I'm saying is, I know my worth. I know I can out-work most guys half my age, even now. Honestly, if anything, I get a little impatient with people who *don't* know how to do things, or who just hire everything out. It’s like, come on, pick up a hammer, figure it out! So no, I don't walk around feeling less than anybody else. I’m just a guy who likes to get things done, and I know I’m damn good at getting things done. It’s just... getting myself to stop doing things that’s the problem right now. But inferior? No way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you often had trouble following rules or respecting other people’s boundaries in relationships or at work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble with rules or boundaries. I mean, in construction, you *have* to follow rules. If you don't follow the building codes, the inspector comes down on you like a ton of bricks, and then you're tearing out a week's worth of work just because you didn't space the studs right or you used the wrong gauge wire. So I've always respected the rules of the trade. You have to. It's about safety, it's about doing the job right. 

Now, did I always agree with every single rule the foreman laid down? Well, no. Sometimes you get a guy running the site who thinks he knows better than the guys actually swinging the hammers. Like this one time, back in the late nineties, we were working on this big commercial build, and the site manager—this young kid, fresh out of some management program, probably never got a blister in his life—he decides we all need to take our lunch breaks at exactly 12:00 PM. Not 11:45, not 12:15, but 12:00 sharp. And I told him, I said, "Look, if I'm in the middle of pouring concrete, I can't just drop my trowel at noon because your clipboard says so. The concrete doesn't care what time it is." We went round and round about it. But I wasn't breaking the rules just to be a jerk, I was just trying to be practical. 

And as for boundaries in relationships... well, my wife might tell you I have a habit of taking over her projects. Like, she'll start painting a room, and I'll come in and say, "Oh, you're not cutting in the corners right, let me show you," and before you know it, I've got the roller in my hand and she's gone to read a book in the living room. But I'm just trying to help, you know? I just want it done right and done fast. I don't think that's disrespecting boundaries, it's just... well, it's just me wanting to get the job finished. I've never been the type to step on people's toes intentionally or, you know, get into trouble with the law or anything like that. I'm a straight shooter. Always have been.

First I'm out cleaning the gutters, then I'm in the garage reorganizing the workbenches, then I'm fixing a squeaky hinge on the back gate that wasn't even really bothering anybody. \n\nIt’s just... it's like I still have a crew waiting on me, you know? She came home from the grocery store and just about had a fit because she thought I took off too many branches on the left side. But I told her, \"Look, that branch was getting too close to the power lines, and it's better to be safe than sorry.\" So, no, I definitely don't need reassurance. I just make a decision and go with it, for better or worse. That's never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember, or did this start more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I’ve always been a guy who likes to keep busy, always had a lot of energy, you know? Like I said, running a crew, you gotta be moving. But... this specific thing, this feeling like I'm just spinning my wheels and can't turn the engine off? That's newer. I'd say it really became a real problem, like, a noticeable issue where I'm driving myself and my wife up the wall, after I retired. \n\nI hung up my toolbelt—well, my professional toolbelt, anyway, I still got my own out in the garage—about a year and two months ago. It was late October, I remember because we had just finished up a big siding job over on Elm Street right before the first frost hit. And I thought, \"That's it, I'm done. Time to relax.\" But almost right away, maybe a couple weeks in, once the novelty of not having an alarm clock wore off, that's when this really kicked into high gear. It's like, without the job to channel all that into, it just started spilling out everywhere else. I mean, managing things on my own is basically what I've done my whole life. When you're out there on a job site, you can't be wringing your hands worrying if you can handle it. You just handle it. You figure out how many yards of concrete you need, you make sure the framing is plumb, you deal with the inspector when he shows up in a bad mood... you just do it. \n\nEven now, around the house, I'm not sitting there worrying if I can manage. If the lawnmower won't start, I don't panic and think, \"Oh no, I can't do this.\" I just pull the spark plug, check the carburetor, maybe run out to the hardware store for some fresh gas or a new filter. I mean, I might get frustrated if I have to take the whole housing apart just to get to one little bolt—they don't make these things like they used to, let me tell you, it's all plastic clips now that snap if you look at them wrong—but I don't worry that I *can't* do it. \n\nI guess the problem isn't worrying about managing things, it's that I'm trying to manage *too many* things all at once because I can't just leave well enough alone. My wife says I'm managing things that don't need managing. Like last week, I decided to reorganize the entire basement storage area. Did it need doing? Maybe not right then. But I just started pulling boxes out, sorting through old holiday decorations, tossing out stuff we haven't used since the kids were in grade school. I had a whole system going. So, no, I definitely don't worry about being able to handle things on my own. I've always been independent that way.\n\nDoctor: Since retiring, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Not depressed, anyway. Frustrated, maybe, like I said, because I just can't seem to get comfortable doing nothing, but not sad. \n\nI mean, I look at some of the guys I used to work with who retired before me. Take Charlie, for instance. He retired about five years ago, and within six months, he was just sitting in his recliner all day watching cable news, getting all worked up about politics, and just looking miserable. I went over to his place to help him fix a leaky pipe under his sink—he never was any good at plumbing, always a framing guy—and he just looked so defeated, you know? Like all the wind was out of his sails. \n\nI'm not like that. I wake up, and I want to go, go, go. I want to build something, fix something, tear something down and put it back together better. My mood is fine, honestly. Sometimes I even wake up in a great mood because I've got a whole list of projects in my head. Like yesterday, I woke up thinking about how I could build a better shelving unit for my wife's gardening supplies out back. I had the whole thing sketched out on a napkin by the time she came down for coffee. So, no, I'm not sitting around crying into my oatmeal or feeling hopeless. It's more like... I've got all this energy and nowhere productive to put it, which drives me a little crazy, sure, but I'm definitely not depressed. I just need a project. Always need a project.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel inferior to other people?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. Inferior? Nah. I mean, look, I've worked hard my whole life. I know what I bring to the table, you know? When you've spent forty years in construction, you learn pretty quick that everybody's got their own set of skills. \n\nLike, take my brother-in-law, Dave. Dave's an accountant. He's got all these fancy degrees, wears a suit to work, the whole nine yards. And sure, if you put me in front of a spreadsheet, I'd probably be lost in five minutes. But if Dave's roof starts leaking, or his deck starts sagging? He's calling me. He doesn't know a joist hanger from a hole in the ground. So, do I feel inferior to Dave because he makes more money or went to college? Absolutely not. We just do different things. \n\nI've always been proud of the work I do. I built my own garage from the ground up, poured the slab myself, framed it, wired it—well, I had a buddy check the electrical, just to be safe, but I did the work. You don't do something like that and feel inferior to the guy next door just because he drives a newer truck. \n\nI guess what I'm saying is, I know my worth. I know I can out-work most guys half my age, even now. Honestly, if anything, I get a little impatient with people who *don't* know how to do things, or who just hire everything out. It’s like, come on, pick up a hammer, figure it out! So no, I don't walk around feeling less than anybody else. I’m just a guy who likes to get things done, and I know I’m damn good at getting things done. It’s just... getting myself to stop doing things that’s the problem right now. But inferior? No way.\n\nDoctor: Have you often had trouble following rules or respecting other people’s boundaries in relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble with rules or boundaries. I mean, in construction, you *have* to follow rules. If you don't follow the building codes, the inspector comes down on you like a ton of bricks, and then you're tearing out a week's worth of work just because you didn't space the studs right or you used the wrong gauge wire. So I've always respected the rules of the trade. You have to. It's about safety, it's about doing the job right. \n\nNow, did I always agree with every single rule the foreman laid down? Well, no. Sometimes you get a guy running the site who thinks he knows better than the guys actually swinging the hammers. Like this one time, back in the late nineties, we were working on this big commercial build, and the site manager—this young kid, fresh out of some management program, probably never got a blister in his life—he decides we all need to take our lunch breaks at exactly 12:00 PM. But I wasn't breaking the rules just to be a jerk, I was just trying to be practical. \n\nAnd as for boundaries in relationships... well, my wife might tell you I have a habit of taking over her projects. Like, she'll start painting a room, and I'll come in and say, \"Oh, you're not cutting in the corners right, let me show you,\" and before you know it, I've got the roller in my hand and she's gone to read a book in the living room. But I'm just trying to help, you know? I just want it done right and done fast. I don't think that's disrespecting boundaries, it's just... well, it's just me wanting to get the job finished. I've never been the type to step on people's toes intentionally or, you know, get into trouble with the law or anything like that. I'm a straight shooter. Always have been.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince retiring, have you felt depressed or down most days?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Wait, didn't you just ask me that? About feeling down or depressed? I'm pretty sure we just went over that, right? 

But, no, like I was saying before, I'm really not depressed. I mean, sure, I have my moments where I get a little stir-crazy, but that's not the same thing as being sad or, you know, moping around. It's more like... well, it's like having a sports car stuck in traffic. The engine is running, you want to open it up on the highway, but you're just sitting there idling. That's how retirement feels sometimes. Just idling. 

But depressed? No. I keep busy. Or I try to, anyway. Like yesterday, after I finished sketching out those shelves for the gardening supplies, I ended up going down to the hardware store just to look at lumber prices. Spent an hour talking to the guy in the lumber yard—Hank, I think his name was, nice guy, used to work framing houses out in the suburbs before his knees gave out. We got to talking about the price of oriented strand board these days, and let me tell you, it's daylight robbery compared to what it was ten years ago. Anyway, I came back home, didn't even buy anything, but I felt fine. I felt energized just talking shop. 

So if you're asking if I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself because I'm not on a job site anymore, the answer is no. I miss the work, sure, I miss the guys, I miss the routine. But I'm not down in the dumps. I'm just... looking for the next thing to sink my teeth into, you know? It's just a matter of finding the right project to keep this engine running.
=====================================

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.7, F31.9, F43.21\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, nice to meet you, Doc. I guess... well, to be honest, it’s a bit of a story. You see, I retired recently—finally decided it was time to step away after all those years—and everybody always tells you that retirement is about taking it easy, right? Sitting on the porch, maybe doing a little fishing, just enjoying the quiet. \n\nBut what brings me in is that since I stopped working, I just... I can’t seem to slow down. Not even a little bit. I wake up and I'm just up and moving from one little job to the next all day long. First I'm out cleaning the gutters, then I'm in the garage reorganizing the workbenches, then I'm fixing a squeaky hinge on the back gate that wasn't even really bothering anybody. \n\nIt’s just... it's like I still have a crew waiting on me, you know? Like if I don't keep moving and getting things done, the whole site is gonna fall behind schedule. But there is no schedule anymore! I just go and go and go, and I can't seem to just sit in my chair for ten minutes without feeling like I need to be up doing something. It's driving me a little crazy, to be honest with you, so I figured I better come in and talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually upbeat, overly energetic, or much more irritable than is normal for you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I've been feeling unusually upbeat or anything like that. It's not like I'm walking around on cloud nine or feeling like I won the lottery. And as for being overly energetic... well, I guess that's the tricky part, isn't it? Because like I said, I'm always on the move, always finding some project to tackle. But it's not this... this burst of extra energy, you know? It's just my normal speed. It's how I've always been, just... go, go, go. \n\nAnd irritable? Well, my wife might tell you a different story if she was sitting here, but honestly, I don't think I'm any more irritable than usual. Sure, I get frustrated when things don't go right, like when I was trying to fix that hinge and the screws were all stripped out. I might have muttered a few choice words then, but who wouldn't? It's not like I'm snapping at people for no reason or flying off the handle over every little thing. I'm just... well, I'm just me, you know? Just trying to stay busy and get things done, even if there really isn't anything that *needs* getting done. It's just a restless feeling, not like I'm suddenly a different person or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to make everyday decisions without getting advice or reassurance from other people?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. Actually, if anything, I'm probably the opposite. I've always been the guy making the calls, you know? When you spend thirty-some years running a crew, you can't be standing around waiting for someone else to tell you what size nail to use or whether to pour the concrete today or tomorrow. You just look at the sky, look at the plans, and you make the call. \n\nEven now around the house, I don't need my wife to tell me how to organize the garage or what color to paint the trim. I just go ahead and do it. Sometimes that gets me in a little hot water, I suppose—like last week when I decided to trim back the oak tree in the front yard without mentioning it to her first. She came home from the grocery store and just about had a fit because she thought I took off too many branches on the left side. But I told her, \"Look, that branch was getting too close to the power lines, and it's better to be safe than sorry.\" So, no, I definitely don't need reassurance. I just make a decision and go with it, for better or worse. That's never been a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember, or did this start more recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I’ve always been a guy who likes to keep busy, always had a lot of energy, you know? Like I said, running a crew, you gotta be moving. But... this specific thing, this feeling like I'm just spinning my wheels and can't turn the engine off? That's newer. I'd say it really became a real problem, like, a noticeable issue where I'm driving myself and my wife up the wall, after I retired. \n\nI hung up my toolbelt—well, my professional toolbelt, anyway, I still got my own out in the garage—about a year and two months ago. It was late October, I remember because we had just finished up a big siding job over on Elm Street right before the first frost hit. And I thought, \"That's it, I'm done. Time to relax.\" But almost right away, maybe a couple weeks in, once the novelty of not having an alarm clock wore off, that's when this really kicked into high gear. It's like, without the job to channel all that into, it just started spilling out everywhere else. So yeah, I've always been a go-getter, but this... this inability to just sit still, this is really since I retired a year and two months ago.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about being unable to manage things on your own?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, managing things on my own is basically what I've done my whole life. When you're out there on a job site, you can't be wringing your hands worrying if you can handle it. You just handle it. You figure out how many yards of concrete you need, you make sure the framing is plumb, you deal with the inspector when he shows up in a bad mood... you just do it. \n\nEven now, around the house, I'm not sitting there worrying if I can manage. If the lawnmower won't start, I don't panic and think, \"Oh no, I can't do this.\" I just pull the spark plug, check the carburetor, maybe run out to the hardware store for some fresh gas or a new filter. I mean, I might get frustrated if I have to take the whole housing apart just to get to one little bolt—they don't make these things like they used to, let me tell you, it's all plastic clips now that snap if you look at them wrong—but I don't worry that I *can't* do it. \n\nI guess the problem isn't worrying about managing things, it's that I'm trying to manage *too many* things all at once because I can't just leave well enough alone. My wife says I'm managing things that don't need managing. Like last week, I decided to reorganize the entire basement storage area. Did it need doing? Maybe not right then. But I just started pulling boxes out, sorting through old holiday decorations, tossing out stuff we haven't used since the kids were in grade school. I had a whole system going. So, no, I definitely don't worry about being able to handle things on my own. I've always been independent that way.\n\nDoctor: Since retiring, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I've been feeling down. Not depressed, anyway. Frustrated, maybe, like I said, because I just can't seem to get comfortable doing nothing, but not sad. \n\nI mean, I look at some of the guys I used to work with who retired before me. Take Charlie, for instance. He retired about five years ago, and within six months, he was just sitting in his recliner all day watching cable news, getting all worked up about politics, and just looking miserable. I went over to his place to help him fix a leaky pipe under his sink—he never was any good at plumbing, always a framing guy—and he just looked so defeated, you know? Like all the wind was out of his sails. \n\nI'm not like that. I wake up, and I want to go, go, go. I want to build something, fix something, tear something down and put it back together better. My mood is fine, honestly. Sometimes I even wake up in a great mood because I've got a whole list of projects in my head. Like yesterday, I woke up thinking about how I could build a better shelving unit for my wife's gardening supplies out back. I had the whole thing sketched out on a napkin by the time she came down for coffee. So, no, I'm not sitting around crying into my oatmeal or feeling hopeless. It's more like... I've got all this energy and nowhere productive to put it, which drives me a little crazy, sure, but I'm definitely not depressed. I just need a project. Always need a project.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel inferior to other people?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. Inferior? Nah. I mean, look, I've worked hard my whole life. I know what I bring to the table, you know? When you've spent forty years in construction, you learn pretty quick that everybody's got their own set of skills. \n\nLike, take my brother-in-law, Dave. Dave's an accountant. He's got all these fancy degrees, wears a suit to work, the whole nine yards. And sure, if you put me in front of a spreadsheet, I'd probably be lost in five minutes. But if Dave's roof starts leaking, or his deck starts sagging? He's calling me. He doesn't know a joist hanger from a hole in the ground. So, do I feel inferior to Dave because he makes more money or went to college? Absolutely not. We just do different things. \n\nI've always been proud of the work I do. I built my own garage from the ground up, poured the slab myself, framed it, wired it—well, I had a buddy check the electrical, just to be safe, but I did the work. You don't do something like that and feel inferior to the guy next door just because he drives a newer truck. \n\nI guess what I'm saying is, I know my worth. I know I can out-work most guys half my age, even now. Honestly, if anything, I get a little impatient with people who *don't* know how to do things, or who just hire everything out. It’s like, come on, pick up a hammer, figure it out! So no, I don't walk around feeling less than anybody else. I’m just a guy who likes to get things done, and I know I’m damn good at getting things done. It’s just... getting myself to stop doing things that’s the problem right now. But inferior? No way.\n\nDoctor: Have you often had trouble following rules or respecting other people’s boundaries in relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble with rules or boundaries. I mean, in construction, you *have* to follow rules. If you don't follow the building codes, the inspector comes down on you like a ton of bricks, and then you're tearing out a week's worth of work just because you didn't space the studs right or you used the wrong gauge wire. So I've always respected the rules of the trade. You have to. It's about safety, it's about doing the job right. \n\nNow, did I always agree with every single rule the foreman laid down? Well, no. Sometimes you get a guy running the site who thinks he knows better than the guys actually swinging the hammers. Like this one time, back in the late nineties, we were working on this big commercial build, and the site manager—this young kid, fresh out of some management program, probably never got a blister in his life—he decides we all need to take our lunch breaks at exactly 12:00 PM. Not 11:45, not 12:15, but 12:00 sharp. And I told him, I said, \"Look, if I'm in the middle of pouring concrete, I can't just drop my trowel at noon because your clipboard says so. The concrete doesn't care what time it is.\" We went round and round about it. But I wasn't breaking the rules just to be a jerk, I was just trying to be practical. \n\nAnd as for boundaries in relationships... well, my wife might tell you I have a habit of taking over her projects. Like, she'll start painting a room, and I'll come in and say, \"Oh, you're not cutting in the corners right, let me show you,\" and before you know it, I've got the roller in my hand and she's gone to read a book in the living room. But I'm just trying to help, you know? I just want it done right and done fast. I don't think that's disrespecting boundaries, it's just... well, it's just me wanting to get the job finished. I've never been the type to step on people's toes intentionally or, you know, get into trouble with the law or anything like that. I'm a straight shooter. Always have been.\n\nDoctor: Since retiring, have you felt depressed or down most days?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? About feeling down or depressed? I'm pretty sure we just went over that, right? \n\nBut, no, like I was saying before, I'm really not depressed. I mean, sure, I have my moments where I get a little stir-crazy, but that's not the same thing as being sad or, you know, moping around. It's more like... well, it's like having a sports car stuck in traffic. The engine is running, you want to open it up on the highway, but you're just sitting there idling. That's how retirement feels sometimes. Just idling. \n\nBut depressed? No. I keep busy. Or I try to, anyway. Like yesterday, after I finished sketching out those shelves for the gardening supplies, I ended up going down to the hardware store just to look at lumber prices. Spent an hour talking to the guy in the lumber yard—Hank, I think his name was, nice guy, used to work framing houses out in the suburbs before his knees gave out. We got to talking about the price of oriented strand board these days, and let me tell you, it's daylight robbery compared to what it was ten years ago. Anyway, I came back home, didn't even buy anything, but I felt fine. I felt energized just talking shop. \n\nSo if you're asking if I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself because I'm not on a job site anymore, the answer is no. I miss the work, sure, I miss the guys, I miss the routine. But I'm not down in the dumps. I'm just... looking for the next thing to sink my teeth into, you know? It's just a matter of finding the right project to keep this engine running.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Sometimes you get a guy running the site who thinks he knows better than the guys actually swinging the hammers. Like this one time, back in the late nineties, we were working on this big commercial build, and the site manager—this young kid, fresh out of some management program, probably never got a blister in his life—he decides we all need to take our lunch breaks at exactly 12:00 PM. Not 11:45, not 12:15, but 12:00 sharp. And I told him, I said, \"Look, if I'm in the middle of pouring concrete, I can't just drop my trowel at noon because your clipboard says so. The concrete doesn't care what time it is.\" We went round and round about it. But I wasn't breaking the rules just to be a jerk, I was just trying to be practical. \n\nAnd as for boundaries in relationships... well, my wife might tell you I have a habit of taking over her projects. Like, she'll start painting a room, and I'll come in and say, \"Oh, you're not cutting in the corners right, let me show you,\" and before you know it, I've got the roller in my hand and she's gone to read a book in the living room. But I'm just trying to help, you know? I just want it done right and done fast. I don't think that's disrespecting boundaries, it's just... well, it's just me wanting to get the job finished. I've never been the type to step on people's toes intentionally or, you know, get into trouble with the law or anything like that. I'm a straight shooter. Always have been.\n\nDoctor: Since retiring, have you felt depressed or down most days?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? About feeling down or depressed? I'm pretty sure we just went over that, right? \n\nBut, no, like I was saying before, I'm really not depressed. I mean, sure, I have my moments where I get a little stir-crazy, but that's not the same thing as being sad or, you know, moping around. It's more like... well, it's like having a sports car stuck in traffic. The engine is running, you want to open it up on the highway, but you're just sitting there idling. That's how retirement feels sometimes. Just idling. \n\nBut depressed? No. I keep busy. Or I try to, anyway. Like yesterday, after I finished sketching out those shelves for the gardening supplies, I ended up going down to the hardware store just to look at lumber prices. Spent an hour talking to the guy in the lumber yard—Hank, I think his name was, nice guy, used to work framing houses out in the suburbs before his knees gave out. We got to talking about the price of oriented strand board these days, and let me tell you, it's daylight robbery compared to what it was ten years ago. Anyway, I came back home, didn't even buy anything, but I felt fine. I felt energized just talking shop. \n\nSo if you're asking if I'm sitting around feeling sorry for myself because I'm not on a job site anymore, the answer is no. I miss the work, sure, I miss the guys, I miss the routine. But I'm not down in the dumps. I'm just... looking for the next thing to sink my teeth into, you know? It's just a matter of finding the right project to keep this engine running.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Early adulthood? Doc, try way before that. I’ve been running on this same motor since I was in knee pants. You ask anyone who knew me back then, they'll tell you. I mean, sure, in my twenties I was working two jobs and remodeling a starter house on the weekends, so I was always on the go, but honestly, it goes all the way back to when I was just a little kid. 

I remember being in, what was it, maybe third grade? Mrs. Gable's class. Strict lady, wore those glasses on a chain around her neck. She used to tell my parents at those parent-teacher conferences—my mom would come home and tell me all about it—she’d say, "He’s a bright boy, but he’s just so scattered." That was the exact word she used. Scattered. Because, well, I was always losing my pencils. I swear, my dad must have bought me a hundred of those yellow Ticonderoga pencils, you know, the good ones with the green and yellow metal around the eraser? And I’d lose them by Tuesday. I'd set them down, start looking at something else, and poof, gone. 

And I'd always be the one blurting things out, too. She’d ask a math question, and I wouldn’t even bother to raise my hand, I’d just yell out the answer. I just couldn't wait for her to call on somebody else, especially if I knew I had it right. And then, you know, when it came time to actually sit down and do the worksheets, I’d get halfway through, get distracted by a bird outside or somebody sharpening a pencil across the room, and she’d have to come over and tap my desk. She was always needing to remind me to finish my work before recess. Over and over. "Got to finish this before you go out to play," she’d say. Because if she didn't remind me, I’d be out that door the second the bell rang, worksheet half empty, already thinking about playing kickball or digging in the dirt. 

I just wanted to be outside, doing things with my hands, moving around. Sitting at a little wooden desk all day was torture. So yeah, early adulthood, sure, but this whole... needing to go, go, go and having trouble just sitting still and focusing on the paperwork part of life? That’s been me my entire life. I don't think I've ever really changed, honestly. I just got bigger tools and bigger projects. But the motor? Same motor since I was seven years old, losing pencils and just trying to get to recess.
